\Gamma\vdash b_{m}\operatorname{\triangleleft}\operatorname{\mathbf{Args}}_{m}(I,i,c)[\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}]}{\Gamma\vdash c^{I}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\operatorname{\triangleright}I\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}(\mathbf{a})}[Cons]\\
\mpr@inferrule\square{\Gamma\vdash s\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{I}}I\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}(\mathbf{a})\\
\Gamma,z:I(\mathbf{a})\vdash P\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square{}}}\square{}_{j}\\
\\
\Gamma,f:(\operatorname{\Pi}z:I\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}(\mathbf{a}).P),\mathbf{y}:\operatorname{\mathbf{Args}}(I,i,c_{k})[\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y}]\vdash t_{k}\operatorname{\triangleleft}P[c^{I}_{k}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y})/z]}{\Gamma\vdash\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(s,z.P,f.\mathbf{y.t})\operatorname{\triangleright}P[s/z]}\textsc{Fix}

\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleleft}T

\mpr@inferrule\square{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleright}T^{\prime}\\
T^{\prime}\equiv T}{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleleft}T}\textsc{Check}

\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\bullet}}T

\mpr@inferrule\square{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleright}T\\
T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B}{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\operatorname{\Pi}}}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B}\textsc{Prod-Inf}\mpr@inferrule\square{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleright}T\\
T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}I\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}(\mathbf{a})}{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{I}}I\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}(\mathbf{a})}\textsc{Ind-Inf}\mpr@inferrule\square{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleright}T\\
T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\square{}_{i}}{\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square{}}}\square{}_{i}}\textsc{Univ-Inf}

t\leadsto u
(congruence rules omitted)

(\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t)~u\leadsto t[u/x]\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(c_{k}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}),z.P,f.\mathbf{y.t})\leadsto t_{k}[\operatorname{\lambda}x:I(\mathbf{a}).\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(x,z.P,f.\mathbf{y.t})/f][\mathbf{b}/\mathbf{y}]

t\equiv u

t\equiv u\quad:=\quad\exists v~v^{\prime},t\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}v\wedge u\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}v^{\prime}\wedge v=_{\alpha}v^{\prime}

where
=_{\alpha}
denotes syntactic equality up-to renaming

Figure 1
.
\mathsf{CIC}
: Bidirectional typing

In the usual, declarative, presentation of
\mathsf{CIC}
, conversion between types is
allowed at any stage of a typing derivation through a free-standing conversion
rule.
However, when conversion is replaced by a non-transitive relation of consistency, this free-standing rule is much too permissive and would violate
\mathcal{C}_{/{\mathsf{CIC}}}
.
Indeed, as every type should be consistent with the unknown type

{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square{}}
, using such a rule twice in a row makes it possible to change the type of a
typable term to any arbitrary type: if
\Gamma\vdash t:T
, because
T\sim{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}

and
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}\sim S
, we could derive
\Gamma\vdash t:S
. This in turn would allow typeability of any term, including fully-precise terms, which is in contradiction with
\mathcal{C}_{/{\mathsf{CIC}}}
.

Thus, we rely on a bidirectional presentation of
\mathsf{CIC}
typing, presented in
Fig.

1
, where the usual judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:T
is decomposed into several mutually-defined judgments. The difference between the judgments lies in the role of the type:
in the
inference
judgment
\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleright}T
, the type is considered an output, whereas in the

checking
judgment
\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\triangleleft}T
, the type is instead seen as an input.
Conversion can then be restricted to specific positions, namely to mediate between
inference and checking judgments (see
1

CIC

: Bidirectional typing
), and can thus never appear
twice in a row.

Additionally, in the framework of an elaboration procedure, it is interesting to make a clear distinction between the subject of the rule (
i.e.,
the object that is to be elaborated), inputs that can be used for this elaboration, and outputs that must be constructed during the elaboration.
In the context checking judgment
\vdash\Gamma
,
\Gamma
is the subject of the judgment. In all the other judgments, the subject is the term, the context is an input, and the type is either an input or an output, as we just explained.

An important discipline, that goes with this distinction, is that judgments should ensure that outputs are well-formed, under the hypothesis that the inputs are. All rules are built to ensure this invariant. This distinction between inputs, subject and output, and the associated discipline, are inspired by
McBride 2018
;
McBride 2019
.
This is also the reason why no rule for term elaboration re-checks the context, as it is an input that is assumed to be well-formed. Hence, most properties we state in an open context involve an explicit hypothesis that the involved context is well-formed.

Constrained Inference

Apart from inference and checking, we also use a set of
constrained inference
judgments
\Gamma\vdash t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\bullet}}T
, with the same modes as inference. These judgments infer the type
T
but under some constraint
\bullet
: for instance that it should be a universe at some level (
\bullet=\square{}
), a
\Pi
-type (
\bullet=\operatorname{\Pi}
), or an instance of an inductive
I
(
\bullet=\text{I}
).
Constrained inference judgments come from a close analysis of typing algorithms, such as the one of
\mathrm{Coq}
, where in some places, an intermediate judgment between inference and checking happens: inference is performed, but then the type is reduced to expose its head constructor, which is imposed to be a specific one.
A stereotypical example is
1

CIC

: Bidirectional typing
: one starts by inferring a type for
t
, but want it to be a
\Pi
-type so that its domain can be used to check
u
.
To the best of our knowledge, these judgments have never been formally described elsewhere.
Instead, in the rare bidirectional presentations of
\mathsf{CIC}
, they are inlined in some way, as they only amount to some reduction.
However, this is no longer true in a gradual setting:
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
introduces an alternative, valid solution to the constrained inference, as a term of type
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
can be used where a term with a
\Pi
-type is expected. Thus, we will need multiple rules for constrained inference, which is why we make it explicit already at this stage.

Reduction

From here on, we impose no reduction strategy by default, and use
\leadsto
and the unqualified word "reduction" for
full
reduction,
i.e.,
reduction that can be performed at an arbitrary place in a term, and
\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}
for its reflexive, transitive closure. Most of the properties would however carry over if we fixed
weak-head
reduction instead, and we sketch at the end of some proofs how they would carry over to such a fixed strategy. As uniqueness of inferred types and elaborated terms becomes stronger with a deterministic reduction strategy, we discuss weak-head reduction specifically in that case.

Finally, we observe that the equivalence of this bidirectional
formulation with standard
\mathsf{CIC}
relies on the transitivity of
conversion; this has been very recently spelled out in details and formalized by

Lennon-Bertrand 2021
.
However, in the gradual setting, this property does not hold. This is precisely the point of
using a bidirectional formulation: since
consistency is not a transitive relation, a standard
presentation of typing is not appropriate.

5.
From
\mathsf{GCIC}
to
\mathsf{CastCIC}

We now present the elaboration from the source gradual system
\mathsf{GCIC}
to the cast calculus
\mathsf{CastCIC}
.
We start with
\mathsf{CastCIC}
, describing its typing, reduction
and metatheoretical properties (
Section

5.1
).
We next describe
\mathsf{GCIC}
and its elaboration to

\mathsf{CastCIC}
, along with few direct properties (
Section

5.2
). This elaboration is mainly an extension of the bidirectional
\mathsf{CIC}
presented in the previous section.
We illustrate the semantics of the different
\mathsf{GCIC}
variants by considering the
\Omega
term (
Section

5.3
).
We finally expose technical properties
of the reduction of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
(
Section

5.4
) used to prove the most
important theorems on elaboration: conservativity over
\mathsf{CIC}
or
\mathsf{CIC}^{\uparrow}
, as well as
the gradual guarantees (
Section

5.5
).

5.1.
\mathsf{CastCIC}

Syntax

The syntax of
\mathsf{CastCIC}

10
10

10

Written using a
blue color
.

extends that of
\mathsf{CIC}
(
Section

4
)
with three new term constructors: the unknown term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{T}
and dynamic
error
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{T}
of type
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
, as well as the cast
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{T}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{S}\rangle\,{t}
of a term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
of type
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S
to type
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T

(Syntax of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{Term}_{\mathsf{CastCIC}}\ni\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t::=\dots\mid\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{t}\mid\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{t}\mid\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{t}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{t}\rangle\,{t}

with casts associating to the right:
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{S^{\prime}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{S}\rangle\,{\langle{T}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T^{\prime}}\rangle\,{t}}
is
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{S^{\prime}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{S}\rangle\,{\left(\langle{T^{\prime}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{t}\right)}
. We also compress successive ones in the following way:
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{T^{\prime\prime}\Leftarrow T^{\prime}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{t}
is shorthand for
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{T^{\prime\prime}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T^{\prime}}\rangle\,{\langle{T^{\prime}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{t}}
.
The unknown term and dynamic error both behave as exceptions as
defined in
\mathsf{ExTT}

(
Pédrot and
Tabareau 2018
)
.
Casts keep track of the use of consistency during elaboration, implementing
a form of runtime type-checking, raising the error
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{T}
in case of a type mismatch.
We call
static
the terms of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
that do not use any of these new constructors—static
\mathsf{CastCIC}
terms correspond to
\mathsf{CIC}
terms.

Universe parameters

(
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
-
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}

)

\displaystyle\qquad s_{\Pi}(i,j)

\displaystyle:=\max(i,j)

\displaystyle c_{\Pi}(i)

\displaystyle:=i

(
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
-
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}

)

\displaystyle s_{\Pi}(i,j)

\displaystyle:=\max(i,j)

\displaystyle c_{\Pi}(i)

\displaystyle:=i-1

(
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
-
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}

)

\displaystyle s_{\Pi}(i,j)

\displaystyle:=\max(i,j)+1

\displaystyle c_{\Pi}(i)

\displaystyle:=i-1

Figure 2
.
Universe parameters

\mathsf{CastCIC}
is parametrized by two functions, described in
Fig.

2
, to account for the three
different variants of
\mathsf{GCIC}
we consider (
Section

3.1
).
The first function
s_{\Pi}
computes
the level of the universe of a dependent product, given
the levels of its domain and codomain (see the updated
3

CastCIC

: Bidirectional typing (extending

CIC

,
replacing )
rule in
Fig.

3
). The second function

c_{\Pi}
controls the universe level in the reduction of a cast between

{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\rightarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
and
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
(see
Fig.

5
).

Typing

Fig.

3
gives the typing rules for the three new primitives of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
. Apart from the modified
3

CastCIC

: Bidirectional typing (extending

CIC

,
replacing )
rule, which uses the
s_{\Pi}
parameter, all other typing rules are exactly the same as in
\mathsf{CIC}
. When disambiguation is needed, we note this typing judgment as
\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}
.
The typing rules

Note that in

Instead, we rely on the usual conversion, defined as
in
\mathsf{CIC}
as the existence of
\alpha

described hereafter.

Head constructor and germ

The typing rules provide little insight on the new primitives; the interesting
part really lie in their reduction behavior. The reduction rules of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
are given in

Fig.

5
(congruence rules omitted).
Reduction relies on two auxiliary functions relating head constructors
h\in\operatorname{Head}
(
Fig.

4
) to those terms that start with either
\Pi
,
\square
or
I
, the set of which we call
\operatorname{Type}_{\mathsf{CastCIC}}
.
The first is the function
\mathrm{head}\,
, which returns the head constructor of a type.
In the other direction, the germ
11
11

11

The germ function corresponds to an abstraction function as in
AGT
(
Garcia
et al. 2016
)
, if one interprets the head
h
as the set of all
types whose head type constructor is
h
.
Wadler and
Findler 2009

christened the corresponding notion a
ground type
, later reused in the
gradual typing literature. This terminology however clashes with
its prior use in denotational semantics
(
Levy 2004
)
: there a ground type
is a first-order datatype. Note that
Siek and Taha 2006
also call ground types the base types of the language, such as
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{N}
. We therefore prefer the less overloaded term
germ
, used by analogy with the
geometrical notion of the
germ of a section

(
MacLane and
Moerdijk 1992
)
: the
germ of a head constructor represents an equivalence class of types that are
locally the same.

function

\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h
constructs the least
precise type with head
h
at level
i
.
In the case where no such type exists (
e.g.,
when
c_{\Pi}(i)<0
),
this least precise type is the error.

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\leadsto\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t

\text{~}\hfill\text{when $\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B\neq\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{j}\,\Pi$ for $j\geq i$}\\

\textsc{Size-Err}:\;\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{A}\rangle\,{t}\leadsto\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}\hfill\text{when $\min\{j\mathop{|}\exists h\in\operatorname{Head},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{j}\,h=A\}>i$}

The design of the reduction rules is mostly dictated by the discrete
and monotone models of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
presented later
in
Section

6
.
Nevertheless, we now provide some intuition about their meaning.
Let us start with rules
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
,
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
,
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
. These rules specify the exception-like propagation behavior of both
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}

and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}
at product and inductive types.
Rules
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)

similarly propagate
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}
when cast between the same inductive
type, and rules
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
do the same
from the unknown type to any type
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}X
.

Next are rules
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
,
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
,
which correspond to success cases of dynamic checks, where the cast is between types with the same head.
In that case, casts are either completely
erased when possible, or propagated. As usual in gradual typing, directly inspired by higher-order contracts
(
Findler and
Felleisen 2002
)
,
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
distributes the function cast in two casts, one for the argument and one for the body; note the substitution in the source codomain in order to account for dependency.
Also, because constructors and inductives are fully-applied,
this
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
rule cannot be blocked on a partially-applied constructor or inductive.
Regarding inductive types, the restriction to reduce only on constructors means that a cast between
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{N}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{N}
is blocked until its argument term is a constructor, rather than disappearing right away as for the universe.
We follow this somewhat non-optimal strategy to be consistent
between inductive types, because for more complex inductive types such as lists, the propagation of casts on subterms cannot be avoided.

On the contrary, rule
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
specifies failure of a dynamic check when the considered types have different heads. Similarly, rules
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
,
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
specify that cast to or from the error type is always an error.

Finally, there are specific rules pertaining to casts to and from
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
, showcasing its behaviour as a universal type. Rules
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
decompose an upcast into
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
as an upcast to a germ followed by an upcast from the germ to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
. This decomposition of an upcast to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
into a series of "atomic" upcasts from a germ to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
is a consequence of the way the cast operation is implemented in
Section

6
, but similar decompositions appear e.g. in
Siek
et al. 2015
, where the equivalent of our germs are called ground types.
The side conditions guarantee that this rule is used when no other applies.
Rule
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
erases the succession of an upcast to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
and a downcast from it. Note that in this rule the upcast
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{\operatorname{germ}_{h}\,i}\rangle\,{t}
works like a constructor for
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{X}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}\rangle\,{}
as a destructor—a view reflected by the canonical and neutral forms of
Fig.

7
for
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}
.
12
12

12

In a simply-typed language such as GTLC
(
Siek
et al. 2015
)
,
where there are no neutrals at the type level,
casts from a germ/ground type
to the unknown type are usually interpreted as tagged values
(
Siek and Taha 2006
)
.
Here, these correspond exactly to the canonical forms of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}
, but we also have to account for the many neutral forms that appear in open contexts.

Finally, rule
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
corresponds to a peculiar kind of error, which only happens due to the presence of a type hierarchy:

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}?_{\square_{i}}
is only universal with respect to types at level
i
, and so a type might
be of a level too high to fit into it. To detect such a case, we check whether
A
is a germ for a level that is below
i
, and when not throw an error.

Meta-Theoretical Properties

The typing and reduction rules just given ensure two of the
meta-theoretical properties introduced in
Section

2
:

\mathcal{S}
for the three variants of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
, as well as
\mathcal{N}
for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
and

\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
.
Before turning to these properties, let us establish a crucial lemma, namely the
confluence of the rewriting system induced by reduction.

Lemma 1 (Confluence of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
).

If
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u
are related by the symmetric, reflexive, transitive closure
of
\leadsto
, then there exists
s
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
.

We extend the notion of parallel reduction (
\Rrightarrow
) for
\mathsf{CIC}
from
(
Sozeau et al. 2020
)
to account for our additional reduction
rules and show that the triangle property—the existence, for any
term
t
, of an optimal reduced term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rho(t)
in one step (
Fig.

6(a)
)—still
holds. From the triangle property, it is
easy to deduce confluence of parallel reduction in one step
(
Fig.

6(b)
), which implies confluence
because parallel reduction is between one-step reduction and
iterated reductions.
This proof method is basically an extension of the Tait-Martin Löf criterion on
parallel reduction
(
Barendregt 1984
;
Takahashi 1995
)
.

\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rho(t)}

(a)

The triangle property.

\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rho(t)}

(b)

The triangle property implies confluence.

Figure 6
.
Representation of the triangle property (left) and its
consequence on confluence (right).

∎

Let us now turn to
\mathcal{S}
, which we prove using the standard
progress and subject reduction properties
(
Wright and
Felleisen 1994
)
.
Progress describes a set of canonical forms, asserting that all terms that do not belong to
such canonical forms are not in normal form,
i.e.,
can take at least
one reduction step.
Fig.

7
provides the definition
of canonical forms, considering head reduction.

As standard in dependent type theories,
we distinguish between canonical forms and neutral
terms. Neutral terms
correspond to (blocked) destructors, waiting for a substitution to
happen, while other canonical forms correspond to constructors.
Additionally, the notion of neutral terms naturally induces a weak-head
reduction strategy that consists in either applying a top-level
reduction or reducing the (only) argument of the
top-level destructor that is in a neutral position.

The canonical forms for plain
\mathsf{CIC}
are given by the first three lines of
Fig.

7
.
The added rules deal with
errors, unknown terms and casts.
First, an error
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{t}
or an unknown term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{t}
is neutral when

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is neutral, and is canonical only when
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square
or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})
,
but not a
\Pi
-type. This is because exception-like terms reduce on

\Pi
-types
(
Pédrot and
Tabareau 2018
)
.
Second, there is an additional specific form of canonical
inhabitants of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}
: these are upcasts from a germ, which can be
seen as a term tagged with the head constructor of its type, in a
matter reminiscent of actual implementations of dynamic typing using
type tags. As we explained when presenting
Fig.

5
,
these canonical forms work as constructors for
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}
.
Finally, the cast operation behaves as a destructor on the
universe
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square
—as if it were an inductive type of usual
\mathsf{CIC}
.
This destructor first scrutinizes the source type of
the cast. This is why the cast is neutral as soon as its source type
is neutral. When the source type reduces to a head constructor, there are two
possibilities. Either that constructor is
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}
, in which case the cast
scrutinizes its argument to be a canonical form
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{t}\rangle\,{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}
and is neutral when this is not the case.
In all other cases, it first scrutinizes the target type, so the cast is neutral when the target type is neutral.
Finally, when both types have head constructors, the cast
might still need its argument to be either a
\lambda
-abstraction or an inductive
constructor to reduce.

Figure 7
.
Head neutral and canonical forms for
\mathsf{CastCIC}

Equipped with the notion of canonical forms, we can state
\mathcal{S}
for
\mathsf{CastCIC}
:

Theorem 2 (Safety of the three variants of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
(
\mathcal{S}
)).

\mathsf{CastCIC}
enjoys:

Progress::

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is a well-typed term of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
, then either
\operatorname{canonical}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
or there is some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\leadsto\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
.

Subject reduction::

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\leadsto{}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}\operatorname{\triangleleft}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A
.

Thus
\mathsf{CastCIC}
enjoys
\mathcal{S}
.

Progress::

The proof is by induction on the typing derivation of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
.
As standard, we show that in all cases,
either a reduction on a subterm happens,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
itself reduces
because some canonical form was not neutral and creates a redex, or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is neutral.

Subject reduction::

Subject reduction can be derived from the injectivity of type
constructors, which is a direct consequence of confluence. See

(
Sozeau et al. 2020
)
for a detailed account of this result in the
simpler setting of
\mathsf{CIC}
.

∎

We now establish normalization of
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
, although the proof below
relies on the discrete model defined in
Section

6.1
.

Theorem 3 (Normalization of
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
(
\mathcal{N}
)).

Every reduction path for a well-typed term in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
or
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
is finite.

The translation induced by the discrete model presented
in
Section

6.1
maps each reduction step to at least one
step (
Theorem

1
). So strong normalization holds
because the target calculus of the translation is normalizing.
∎

5.2.
Elaboration from
\mathsf{GCIC}
to
\mathsf{CastCIC}

Now that
\mathsf{CastCIC}
has been described, we move on to
\mathsf{GCIC}
. The typing judgment of
\mathsf{GCIC}
is
defined
by an elaboration judgment from
\mathsf{GCIC}
to
\mathsf{CastCIC}
, based upon
Fig.

1
, augmenting all judgments with an extra output: the elaborated
\mathsf{CastCIC}
term. This definition of typing using elaboration is required because of the intricate interdependency between typing and reduction exposed in
Section

3
.

Syntax

The syntax of
\mathsf{GCIC}

13
13

13

We use
green
for terms of

\mathsf{GCIC}
. To maintain a distinction in the absence of colors, we also
use tildes (
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
) for terms in
\mathsf{GCIC}
in expressions mixing both source and target terms.
extends that of
\mathsf{CIC}
with a single new term constructor
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}
, where
i
is a universe level. From a user perspective, one is not given direct access to the failure and cast primitives, those only arise through uses of
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
.

Consistent conversion

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t

\mpr@inferrule\square{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x}\mpr@inferrule\square{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square{}_{i}\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square{}_{i}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A^{\prime}.t^{\prime}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}B\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}B^{\prime}}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A^{\prime}.B^{\prime}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t~u\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}~u^{\prime}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbf{a}\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbf{a}\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbf{a^{\prime}}\\

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}P\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}P^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbf{t}\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbf{t^{\prime}}}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(s,z.P,f.\mathbf{y.t})\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(s^{\prime},z.P^{\prime},f.\mathbf{y.t^{\prime}})}\\
\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{B^{\prime}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{A^{\prime}}\rangle\,{t^{\prime}}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{B}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{A}\rangle\,{t}\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}}\mpr@inferrule\square{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{T^{\prime}}}\mpr@inferrule\square{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{T}\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}

Figure 8
.
\mathsf{CastCIC}
:
\alpha
-consistency

Before we can describe typing, we should focus on conversion. Indeed, to account for the imprecision introduced by
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
, elaboration employs
consistent conversion
to compare
\mathsf{CastCIC}
terms rather than usual conversion relation.

Definition 0 (Consistent conversion).

Two
\mathsf{CastCIC}
terms are
\alpha
-consistent
, written
\sim_{\alpha}
, if they are in the relation defined by the inductive rules of
Fig.

8
.

Two terms are
consistently convertible
, or simply
consistent
, noted
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s\sim\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
, if and only if there exists
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
.

Thus
\alpha
-consistency is an extension of
\alpha
-equality that takes imprecision into account. Apart from the standard rules making
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
consistent with any term,
\alpha
-consistency optimistically ignores casts, and does not consider errors to be consistent with themselves.
The first point is to prevent casts inserted by the elaboration from disrupting valid conversions, typically between static terms. The second is guided by the idea that if errors are encountered at elaboration already, the term cannot be well behaved, so it must be rejected as early as possible and we should avoid typing it.
The consistency relation is then built upon
\alpha
-consistency in a way totally similar to how conversion in
Figs.

1
and
5
is built upon
\alpha
-equality. Also note that this formulation of consistent conversion makes no assumption of
normalization, and is therefore usable as such in the non-normalizing
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
.

An important property of consistent conversion,
and a necessary condition for the conservativity of
\mathsf{GCIC}
with respect to
\mathsf{CIC}
(
\mathcal{C}_{/{\mathsf{CIC}}}
),
is that it corresponds to conversion on static terms.

Proposition 5 (Properties of consistent conversion).

(1)

Two static terms are consistently convertible if and only if they are convertible in
\mathsf{CIC}
.

(2)

If
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
have a normal form, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s\sim\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is decidable.

(1)
First remark that
\alpha
-consistency between static terms corresponds to
\alpha
-equality of terms. Thus, and because the reduction of static terms in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
is the same as the reduction of
\mathsf{CIC}
, two consistent static terms must reduce to
\alpha
-equal terms, which in turn implies that they are convertible.
Conversely two convertible terms of
\mathsf{CIC}
have a common reduct,
which is
\alpha
-consistent with itself.

(2)
If
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
are normalizing, they have a finite number
of reducts, thus to decide their consistency it is sufficient to check each pair of reducts for the decidable

\alpha
-consistency. Comparing normal forms is not enough, because a term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
might be stuck because of a cast while another one
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
can be
\alpha
-consistent with it and reduce further, so that the normal form of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
are not
\alpha
-consistent while
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
are consistent.
∎

\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\\

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i+1}}}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{\square_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{t}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}}[Inf-Univ?]\\
\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\Pi}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B}\textsc{Inf-Prod}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}\\
c_{\Pi}(i)\geq 0}{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{t}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\Pi}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi}[Inf-Prod?]\\
\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}I(\mathbf{a})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{I}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})}\textsc{Inf-Ind}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,I}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{t}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{I}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,I}\textsc{Inf-Ind?}

Figure 9
.
Type-directed elaboration from
\mathsf{GCIC}
to
\mathsf{CastCIC}

Elaboration from
\mathsf{GCIC}
to
\mathsf{CastCIC}
is given in
Fig.

9
, closely following the bidirectional presentation of
\mathsf{CIC}
(
Fig.

1
) for most rules, simply carrying around the extra elaborated terms. Note that only the subject of the judgment is a
source term
in
\mathsf{GCIC}
;
other inputs (that have already been elaborated), as well as outputs (that are to be constructed), are
target terms
in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
. Let us comment a bit on the specific modifications and additions compared to
Fig.

1
.

The most salient feature of elaboration is the insertion of casts that mediate
between merely consistent but not convertible types.
They of course are needed in the rule
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

where the terms are compared using consistency. But this is not enough: casts also appear in the newly-introduced rules
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

and
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

for constrained inference, where the type
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}?_{\square_{i}}
is replaced by the least precise type of the appropriate universe level
having the constrained head constructor, which is exactly what the
\operatorname{germ}
function gives us. Note that in the case of
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

we could have replaced
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}
with
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{i+1}\,\square_{i}
to make for a presentation similar to the other two rules. The role of these three rules is to ensure that a term of type
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}
can be used as a function, or as a scrutinee of a match, by giving a way to derive constrained inference for such a term.

It is interesting to observe that the rules for constrained elaboration in a gradual setting bear a close resemblance with those described by
Cimini and Siek 2016
, where a matching operator is introduced to verify that an output type can fit into a certain type constructor—either by having that type constructor as head symbol or by virtue of being
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
. Such a form of matching was already present in our static, bidirectional system, because of the presence of reduction in types. In a way, both
Cimini and Siek 2016
and
Lennon-Bertrand 2021
have the same need of separating the inferred type from operations on it to recover its head constructor, and our mixing of both computation and gradual typing makes that need even clearer.

Rule
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

also deserves some explanation:
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}
is elaborated to

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}
, the least precise term of the least precise type of the whole universe
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}
. This avoids unneeded type annotations on
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
in
\mathsf{GCIC}
. Instead, the context is responsible for
inserting the appropriate cast,
e.g.,

\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\mathrel{::}T
elaborates to a term reducing to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{T}
.
We do not drop annotations altogether because of an important property on which
bidirectional
\mathsf{CIC}
is built: any well-formed term should
infer
a type, not just check.
Thus, we must be able to infer a type for
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
. The obvious choice is to have
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
infer
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
, but this
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
is a term of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
, and thus needs a type index.
Because this
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
is used as a type, this index must be
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square
, and the universe level of the source
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
is there to give us the level of this
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square
.
In a real system, this should be handled by
typical ambiguity
,
14
14

14

Typical ambiguity
(
Harper and
Pollack 1991
)
is the possibility to avoid giving explicit
universe levels, letting the system decide whether a consistent assignment
of levels can be found. In
\mathrm{Coq}
, for instance, one almost never has to be explicit about universe levels when writing
Type
.

alleviating the user from the need to give any annotations when using
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
.

Direct properties

As the elaboration rules are completely syntax-directed, they
immediately translate to
an algorithm for elaboration. Coupled with decidability of consistency (
Proposition

5
),
this makes elaboration decidable whenever
\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}
is normalizing;
when
\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}
is not normalizing, the elaboration algorithm might diverge, resulting in only semi-decidability of typing (as in, for instance, Dependent Haskell
(
Eisenberg 2016
)
).

Theorem 6 (Decidability of elaboration).

The relations of inference, checking and partial inference of
Fig.

9
are decidable in
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
. They are semi-decidable in
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
.

Let us now establish two important properties of elaboration that we can
prove at this stage: elaboration is
correct
, insofar as it produces well-typed

\mathsf{CastCIC}
terms, and functional, in the sense that a given
\mathsf{GCIC}
term can
be elaborated to at most one
\mathsf{CastCIC}
term up to conversion.

Theorem 7 (Correctness of elaboration).

The elaboration produces well-typed terms in a well-formed context. Namely, given
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
such that
\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
, we have that:

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\bullet}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\bullet}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
(with
\bullet
denoting
the same index in both derivations);

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\triangleleft}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}
, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleleft}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
.

The proof is by induction on the elaboration derivation,
mutually with similar properties for all typing judgments.
In particular, for checking, we have an extra hypothesis that the
given type is well-formed, as it is an input that should already
have been typed.

Because the bidirectional typing rules of
\mathsf{CIC}
are very similar to the
\mathsf{GCIC}
-to-
\mathsf{CastCIC}
elaboration rules, the induction is mostly routine. Let us point however that the careful design of the bidirectional rules already in
\mathsf{CIC}
regarding the input/output separation is important here. Indeed, we have that inputs to the successive premises of a rule are always well-formed, either as inputs to the conclusion, or thanks to previous premises. In particular, all context extensions
are valid,
i.e.,

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma,x:A
is used only when
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}
, and similarly only well-formed types are used for checking. This ensures that we can always use the induction hypothesis.

The only novel points to consider are the rules where a cast is inserted. For these, we rely on the validity property (an inferred type is always well-typed itself) to ensure that the domain of inserted casts is well-typed, and thus that the casts can be typed.
∎

Because of the absence of a fixed, deterministic reduction strategy, the elaborated term is not unique. Indeed, since a type can be reduced to multiple product types in rule
Fig.

9
, a term can infer multiple, different types, and since those appear later on in casts, the elaborated terms can differ by having different, albeit convertible, types in their casts. We thus state two theorems: one is uniqueness up to conversion, in case full reduction is used. The second is a strengthening if a weak-head reduction strategy is imposed for reduction.

Theorem 8 (Uniqueness of elaboration—Full reduction).

Elaborated terms are convertible:

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\equiv\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\equiv\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\bullet}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\bullet}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\equiv\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\equiv\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\triangleleft}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\triangleleft}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\equiv\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
.

(Recall that conversion
\equiv
in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
is defined (similarly as in
\mathsf{CIC}
) as the existence of
\alpha
-equal reducts for the reduction given in
Fig.

5
.)

Theorem 9 (Uniqueness of elaboration—Weak-head reduction).

If in
Fig.

9
,
\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}
is replaced by weak-head reduction, then elaborated terms are unique:

•

given
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
, there is at most one
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and one
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
;

•

given
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
, there is at most one
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and one
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\bullet}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
;

•

given
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
, there is at most one
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\triangleleft}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
.

Like for
Theorem

7
, those are proven mutually by induction on the typing derivation.

The main argument is that there is always at most one rule that can apply to get a typing conclusion for a given term. This is true for all inference statements because there is exactly one inference rule for each term constructor, and for checking because there is only one rule to derive checking. In those cases simply combining the hypothesis of uniqueness is enough.

For
\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\Pi}}
, by confluence of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
the inferred type cannot at the same time reduce to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square{}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B
, because those do not have a common reduct. Thus, only one of the two rules
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

and
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

can apply. It is enough to conclude for
Theorem

8
, because reducts of convertible types are still convertible. For
Theorem

9
the deterministic reduction strategy ensures that the inferred type is indeed unique, rather than unique up to conversion. The reasoning is similar for the other constrained inference judgments.
∎

5.3.
Illustration: Back to Omega

Now that
\mathsf{GCIC}
has been entirely presented, let us come back to the important example of
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\Omega
, and explain in detail the behavior described in
Section

3.1
for the three
\mathsf{GCIC}
variants.

Recall that
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\Omega
is the
term
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\delta~\delta
, with
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\delta:=\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\operatorname{\lambda}x:{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i+1\lx@text@rbrace}.x~x
. We leave out the casts present in

Sections

2
and
3
, knowing that they will be introduced by
elaboration. We also use
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
at level
i+1
, because

\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i+1\lx@text@rbrace}
, when elaborated as a type, becomes
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T:=\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{\square_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i+1}}}\rangle\,{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i+1}}}}
, such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}
.
For the rest of this section, we write
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{j}
instead of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}

to avoid stacked indices and ease readability.

If
i=0
the elaboration of
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\delta
(and thus of
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\Omega
)
fails in
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
,
because the inferred type for
x
is
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
, which reduces to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{0}
.
Then, because
c_{\Pi}(0)=-1<0
in both
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
, rule
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

does not apply and
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\delta
is deemed ill-typed, as is
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\Omega
.

Otherwise, if
i>0
or we are considering
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\delta
can be elaborated, and we have

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\cdot\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\delta\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:T.\left(\langle{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{x}\right)~\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{c_{\Pi}(i)}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{x}\right)\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{c_{\Pi}(i)}

From this, we get that
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\Omega
also elaborates, namely
(with
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\delta^{\prime}
the elaboration of
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\delta
above)

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\cdot\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\Omega\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\delta^{\prime}~\left(\langle{T}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{c_{\Pi}(i)}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{c_{\Pi}(i)}

Let us now look at the reduction behavior of this elaborated term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Omega^{\prime}
in
the three systems: it reduces seamlessly when
c_{\Pi}(i)=i
(
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
/
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
),
while having
c_{\Pi}(i)<i
makes it fail (
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
/
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
/
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
).
The reduction of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Omega^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Omega^{\prime}&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\operatorname{\lambda}x:{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}.\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{x}\right)~\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T}\rangle\,{x}\right)\right)~\left(\langle{T}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{T\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\operatorname{\lambda}x:{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}.\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{x}\right)~\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{x}\right)\right)\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)~\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)~\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\operatorname{\lambda}x:{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}.\hbox{\pagecolor{pastelgray}$\left\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\right\rangle$}\,{\left(\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{x}\right)~(\hbox{\pagecolor{pastelgray}$\left\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\right\rangle$}\,{x})\right)}\right)~\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\\
\end{array}

The first step is the identity, simply replacing
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Omega^{\prime},c_{\Pi}(i)
and the first occurrence of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\delta^{\prime}
by their definitions.
The second reduces
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}
. In the third, the casted
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\delta{}^{\prime}
is substituted for
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x
by a
\beta
step. Casts are finally simplified using
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
.
At that point, the reduction has almost looped back to the second step, apart from the casts
\left\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\right\rangle
in the first occurrence of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\delta^{\prime}
, which will simply accumulate through reduction, but without hindering divergence.

On the contrary, the normalizing variants have
c_{\Pi}(i)<i
,
and thus share the following reduction path:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Omega^{\prime}&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)~\left(\hbox{\pagecolor{pastelgray}$\left\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\right\rangle$}\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)~\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{\delta^{\prime}}\right)~\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\left(\operatorname{\lambda}x:{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}.\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{\left(\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{x^{\prime}}\right)~\left(\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\rangle\,{x^{\prime}}\right)\right)}\right)~\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\\
&&\text{ where $\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x^{\prime}$ is $\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{x}$}\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\rangle\,{(\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}~\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}})}\\
&\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}\end{array}

The first step corresponds to the first three above, the only difference being the value of
c_{\Pi}(i)
. The reductions however differ in the next step because
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\neq\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi
, so
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
applies before
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
.
For the third step, note that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}\to{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}=\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi
,
so that
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
applies in the rightmost sequence of casts.
The last three steps of reduction then propagate the error by first using
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
,
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
, then the
\beta
rule, and finally
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
,
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
and a last
\beta
step.
At a high-level, the error can be seen as a dynamic universe inconsistency, triggered by the invalid downcast
\left\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i-1}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{i}}\right\rangle
highlighted on the first line.

5.4.
Precision is a simulation for reduction

Establishing the graduality of elaboration—the formulation of
the static gradual guarantee (SGG) in our setting—is no small feat, as it
requires properties about
computations in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
that amount to the
dynamic
gradual guarantee (DGG).
Indeed, to handle
the typing rules for checking and constrained inference, it is
necessary to know how consistency and reduction evolve as a type
becomes less precise.
As already explained in
Section

3.4
, we
cannot directly prove graduality for a syntactic notion of
precision. However, we can still show that this relation is a simulation for reduction. While weaker than graduality, this property implies the DGG and suffices to conclude that graduality of elaboration holds.
The purpose of this section is to establish it.
Our proof is partly inspired by the proof of DGG by

Siek
et al. 2015
.
15
15

15

Lemma 7 in
Siek
et al. 2015
is similar to our
Theorem

16
, and
Fig.

10
draws from their Fig. 9, especially for
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

and
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

. Also, while we do not make them explicit here, Lemmas 8, 10 and 11 also appear in our proofs.

We however had to adapt to the much higher complexity of
\mathsf{CIC}
compared to
\mathsf{STLC}
.
In particular, the presence of computation in the domain and codomain of casts is quite subtle to tame, as we must in general reduce types in a cast before we can reduce the cast itself.
16
16

16

Thus, while
Lemmas

13
and
14
correspond roughly to Lemma 9 in
Siek
et al. 2015
,
Lemmas

11
and
12
are completely novel.

Technically, we need to distinguish between two notions of precision, one for
\mathsf{GCIC}
and one for
\mathsf{CastCIC}
:
(i)
syntactic precision
on terms in
\mathsf{GCIC}
, which corresponds to the
usual syntactic precision of gradual typing
(
Siek
et al. 2015
)
, (ii)

structural precision
on terms in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
, which corresponds
to syntactic precision together with a proper account of casts.
In this section, we concentrate on properties of structural precision in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
.
We only state and discuss the various lemmas and
theorems on a high level, and refer the reader to
Section

B.2
for
the detailed proofs.

Structural precision for
\mathsf{CastCIC}

As emphasized already, the key property we want to establish is that precision is a simulation for reduction,

i.e.,
that less precise terms reduce at least as well as more precise ones. This property guides the quite involved definition we are about to give for structural precision: it is rigid enough to give the induction hypotheses needed to prove simulation, while being lax enough to be a consequence of syntactic precision after elaboration, which is the key point to establish elaboration graduality (
Theorem

22
), our equivalent of the static gradual guarantee.
