Figure 10
.
Structural precision in
\mathsf{CastCIC}

Similarly to
\sim_{\alpha}
, precision can ignore some casts, in order to handle casts that might appear or disappear in one term but not the other
during reduction. But in order to control what
casts can be ignored, we impose some restriction on the types
involved. In particular, we want to ensure that ignored casts
would not have raised an error:
e.g.,
we want to prevent
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}0\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{\mathbb{B}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{\mathbb{N}}\rangle\,{0}
.
Thus the definition of structural precision relies on typing, and to do this we need to record the contexts of the two compared terms. We do so by using double-struck letters to denote contexts where each variable is given two types, writing
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma},x:A\mid A^{\prime}
for context extensions. We use
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{i}
for projections,
i.e.,

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}(\mathbb{\Gamma},x:A\mid A^{\prime})_{1}:=\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1},x:A
, and write
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\mid\Gamma^{\prime}
for the converse pairing operation.

Definition 0 (Structural and definitional precision in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
).

Structural precision
, denoted
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
, is defined in
Fig.

10
, mutually with

definitional precision
,
denoted
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
,
which is its closure by reduction.
We write
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma^{\prime}
for the pointwise extensions of those to contexts.

Although
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
is defined in a stepwise way, it is equivalent to the existence of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}

such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}
and

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}
.
The situation is the same as for consistency (resp. conversion), which
is the closure by reduction of
\alpha
-consistency (resp.
\alpha
-equality).
However, here definitional precision is also used in
the definition of structural precision, in order to permit
computation in types—recall that in a dependently-typed setting
the two types involved in a cast may need to reduce before the cast itself can
reduce—and thus the two notions are mutually defined.

Let us now explain the rules defining structural precision. Diagonal rules are completely structural, apart from the
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

rule, where typing assumptions provide us with the contexts needed to compare the predicates. More interesting are the non-diagonal rules.
First,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{T}
is greater than any term of the ”right type”. This incorporates loss of precision (rule
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

), and accommodates for a small bit of cumulativity (rule
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

). This is needed because of technical reasons linked with possibility to form products between types at different levels.
On the contrary, the error is smaller than any term (rule
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

), even in its extended form on
\Pi
-types (rule
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

), with a typing premise similar to that of rule
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

.
Finally, casts on the right-hand side can be ignored as long as they
are performed on types that are
less
precise than the type of the
term on the left (rule
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

). Dually, casts on the
left-hand side can be ignored as long as they are performed on types
that are
more
precise than the type of the term on the right
(rule
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

).

Catch-up lemmas

The fact that structural precision is a simulation relies on a series of lemmas that all have the same form: under the assumption that a term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
is less precise than a term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
with a known head (
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\lambda
or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}c
), the term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
can be reduced to a term that either has the same head, or is some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
. We call these
catch-up
lemmas, as they enable the less precise term to catch up to the more precise one whose head is already known. Their aim is to ensure that casts appearing in a less precise term never block reduction, as they can always be reduced away.

The lemmas are established in a descending fashion: first, on the
universe in
Lemma

11
, then on other types in

Lemma

12
, and finally on terms, namely on
\lambda
-abstractions in
Lemma

13
and inductive constructors in
Lemma

14
. Each time, the previously proven catch-up lemmas are used to reduce types in casts appearing in the less precise term, apart from
Lemma

11
, where the induction hypothesis of the lemma being proven is used instead.

Lemma 11 (Universe catch-up).

Under the hypothesis that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}
, if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{j}
, either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}
with
i<j
, or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}
.

Lemma 12 (Types catchup).

Under the hypothesis that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}
, we have the following:

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{j}
, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}
and
i\leq j
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{j}
then either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}
and
i\leq j
, or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A^{\prime}.B^{\prime}
for some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}B^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A^{\prime}.B^{\prime}
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{j}
then either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}
and
i\leq j
, or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})
for some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}a^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})
.

Lemma 13 (
\lambda
-abstraction catch-up).

If
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}
, where
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is not an error,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\Pi}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A^{\prime}.B^{\prime}
, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A^{\prime}.t^{\prime}
with
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A^{\prime}.t^{\prime}
.

This holds in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
,
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
, and for terms without
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

Lemma 14 (Constructors and inductive error catch-up).

If
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}c(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}c(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{I}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})
, then either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})}
or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}c(\mathbf{a^{\prime}},\mathbf{b^{\prime}})
with
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}c(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}c(\mathbf{a^{\prime}},\mathbf{b^{\prime}})
.

Similarly, if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{I(\mathbf{a})}\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{I(\mathbf{a})}\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{I}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})
, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})}
with

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})
.

Note that for
Lemma

14
, we need to deal with unknown terms
specifically, which is not necessary for
Lemma

13

because the unknown term in a
\Pi
-type reduces to a
\lambda
-abstraction.

Lemma

13
deserves a more extensive discussion, because it is the critical point where the difference between the three variants of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
manifests. In fact, it does not hold in full generality for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
. Indeed, the fact that
i\leq c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,j))
and
j\leq c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,j))
is used crucially to ensure that casting from a
\Pi
-type into
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
and back does not reduce to an error, given the restrictions on types in
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

. This is the manifestation in the reduction of the embedding-projection property
(
New and Ahmed 2018
)
. In
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
it holds only if one restricts to terms without
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
, where such casts never happen. This is important with regard to conservativity, as elaboration produces terms with casts but without
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
, and
Lemma

13
ensures that for those precision is still a simulation, even in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

Example 15 (Catch-up of
\lambda
-abstraction).

The following term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{i}
illustrates these differences

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{i}:=\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}}\rangle\,{\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x)}

where
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{N}
is taken at the lowest level,
i.e.,
to mean
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{N}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace 0\lx@text@rbrace}
. Such terms appear naturally whenever a loss of precision happens on a function, for instance when elaborating a term like
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\left((\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x))\mathrel{::}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\right)~0
. Now this term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{i}
always reduces to

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi\Leftarrow{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}\Leftarrow\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi\Leftarrow\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}}\rangle\,{\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x)}

and at this point the difference kicks in: if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,\Pi
is
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}
(
i.e.,
if
c_{\Pi}(i)<0
) then the whole term reduces to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}}
. Otherwise, further reductions finally give

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}\left(\langle{\mathbb{N}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{\mathbb{N}\Leftarrow\mathbb{N}}\rangle\,{x}\right)

Although the body is blocked by the variable
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x
, applying the function to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}0
would reduce to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}1
as expected. Let us compare what happens in the three systems.

In all of them, if
i\geq 1
, we have
\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x)\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{i}
via repeated uses of
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

since
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbb{N}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{N}\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{s_{\Pi}(0,0)}
and
s_{\Pi}(0,0)\leq 1\leq i
.
Moreover, also
0\leq i-1\leq c_{\Pi}(i)
and so the reduction is errorless.
Thus
Lemma

13
holds in all three systems when
i\geq 1
.

The difference appears in the specific case where
i=0
.
In
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
, we still have
\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x)\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{0}
, since
s_{\Pi}(0,0)=0\leq i
. In the former,
c_{\Pi}(0)=0
so
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{0}
reduces safely and
Lemma

13
holds. In the latter, however,
c_{\Pi}(0)=-1
,
and so
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{0}
errors even if it is less precise than an errorless term—
Lemma

13
does not hold in that case.
Finally, in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{0}
errors since again
c_{\Pi}(0)=-1
.
However, because
s_{\Pi}(0,0)=1
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t_{0}
is not less precise than
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x)
thanks to the typing restriction in
10Structural precision in

CastCIC

, so this error does not contradict
Lemma

13
.

Note that in an actual implementation with typical ambiguity (
Footnote

14
),
the case where
i=0
would most likely not manifest: elaborating

\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\left((\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x))\mathrel{::}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\right)~0
would produce a fresh
level that could be chosen high enough so as to prevent the error we just described.
Only more involved situations like that of
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\Omega
(
Section

5.3
) would actually exhibit
failures due to universe levels, which are precisely those unavoidable to ensure normalization.

Simulation

We finally come to the main property of this section, the advertised
simulation.
Remark that the simulation property needs to be stated (and proven) mutually for structural and definitional precision, but it is really informative only for
structural precision (definitional precision is somehow a
simulation by construction).

Theorem 16 (Precision is a simulation for reduction).

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
. Then

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u
then there exists
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u^{\prime}
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u
then

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u
.

This holds in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
,

\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
and for terms without
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

The case of definitional precision follows by confluence of
reduction.
For the case of structural precision, the hardest point is to simulate
\beta
and
\iota
redexes—terms of the shape
\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(c(\mathbf{a}),z.P,f.\mathbf{y}.\mathbf{t})
. This is where we use
Lemmas

13
and
14
, to show that similar reductions can also happen in
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
.
We must also put some care into handling the premises of precision where typing is involved. In particular, subject reduction is needed to relate the types inferred after reduction to the type inferred before, and the mutual induction hypothesis on
\sqsubseteq_{\leadsto}
is used to conclude that the premises holding on
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
still hold on
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t^{\prime}
.
Finally, the restriction to terms without
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
similar to
Lemma

13
appears again when treating
5

CastCIC

: Reduction rules (extending ,
congruence rules omitted)
, where having
c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,i))=i
is required.
∎

From this theorem, we get as direct corollaries the following properties, that are required to handle reduction (
Corollary

17
) and consistency (
Corollary

18
) in elaboration. Again those corollaries hold in
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
,
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
and for terms in
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
containing no
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
.

Corollary 17 (Monotonicity of reduction to type constructor).

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
be such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square{}_{i}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square{}_{j}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
. Then

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}
then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}
with
i\leq j
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square{}_{i-1}
then either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square{}_{j}}
with
i\leq j
, or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square{}_{i-1}
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B
then either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}
with
i\leq j
, or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A^{\prime}.B^{\prime}
and

\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A^{\prime}.B^{\prime}
;

•

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})
then either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{j}}
with
i\leq j
, or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})
and
\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a})\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})
.

It suffices to simulate the reductions of
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
by using
Theorem

16
, and then use
Lemmas

12
and
11
to conclude. Note that head reductions are simulated using head reductions in
Theorem

16
, and the reductions of
Lemmas

12
and
11
are also head reductions. Thus the corollary still holds when fixing weak-head reduction as a reduction strategy.
∎

Corollary 18 (Monotonicity of consistency).

If
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\sim\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S
then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\sim\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S^{\prime}
.

By definition of
\sim
, we get some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}V
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}V
, and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U\sim_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}V
. By
Theorem

16
,
we can simulate these reductions to get some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}V^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S^{\prime}\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}V^{\prime}
, and also
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}U^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}V\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}V^{\prime}
. Thus we only need to show that
\alpha
-consistency is monotone with respect to structural precision, which is direct by induction on structural precision.
∎

5.5.
Properties of
\mathsf{GCIC}

We now have enough technical tools to prove most of the properties of
\mathsf{GCIC}
.
We state those theorems in an empty context in this section to make
them more readable, but they are of course corollaries of similar
statements including contexts, proven by mutual induction. The complete
statements and proofs can be found in
Section

B.3
.

Conservativity with respect to
\mathsf{CIC}

Elaboration systematically inserts casts during checking, thus even
static terms are not elaborated to themselves. Therefore we use a
(partial) erasure function
\varepsilon
that translates terms of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
to terms of
\mathsf{CIC}
by erasing all casts. We also introduce the notion of erasability, characterizing terms that contain “harmless” casts, such that in particular the elaboration of a static term is always erasable.

Definition 0 (Equiprecision).

Two terms
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
are equiprecise in a context
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}
, denoted
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
if both
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
.

Definition 0 (Erasure, erasability).

Erasure
\varepsilon
is a partial function from the syntax of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
to the syntax of
\mathsf{CIC}
, which is undefined on
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}
, is such that
\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{B}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{A}\rangle\,{t})=\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t)
, and is a congruence for all other term constructors.

Given a context
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}
we say that a term well-typed in
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is erasable if
\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t)
is defined, well-typed in
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{2}
, and equiprecise to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
in
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{\Gamma}
. Similarly a context
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
is called erasable if it is pointwise erasable. When
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
is erasable, we say that a term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is erasable in
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma
to mean that it is erasable in
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\mid\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma)
.

Conservativity holds in all three systems, typeability being of course taken into the corresponding variant of
\mathsf{CIC}
: full
\mathsf{CIC}
for
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
, and
\mathsf{CIC}^{\uparrow}
for
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
.

Theorem 21 (Conservativity).

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
be a static term (
i.e.,
is a term of
\mathsf{GCIC}
that is also a term of
\mathsf{CIC}
).
If
\vdash_{\mathsf{CIC}}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\triangleright}T
for some type
T
,
then there exists
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
such that

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime}
, and
moreover
\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t)=\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
and
\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T^{\prime})=T
. Conversely if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
for some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
, then
\vdash_{\mathsf{CIC}}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\triangleright}\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T)
.

Because
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t
is static, its typing derivation in
\mathsf{GCIC}
can only use rules that have a counterpart in
\mathsf{CIC}
, and conversely all rules of
\mathsf{CIC}
have a counterpart in
\mathsf{GCIC}
. The only difference is about the reduction/conversion side conditions, which are used on elaborated types in
\mathsf{GCIC}
, rather than their non-elaborated counterparts in
\mathsf{CIC}
.

Thus, the main difficulty is to ensure that the extra casts
inserted by elaboration do not alter reduction. For this we
maintain the property that all terms
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
considered in

\mathsf{CastCIC}
are erasable, and in particular that any static term
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{t}

that elaborates to some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
is such that

\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t)=\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{t}
. From the simulation property of
structural precision (
Theorem

16
), we get that an
erasable term
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t
has the same reduction behavior as
its erasure,
i.e.,
if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
then

\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t)\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}s^{\prime}
with
s^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s

equiprecise, and conversely if
\varepsilon(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t)\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}s^{\prime}

then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{{\leadsto}^{\ast}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
with
s^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s

equiprecise. Using that property, we prove that
constraint reductions (
\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\Pi}}
,
\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}
and

\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{I}}
) in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
and
\mathsf{CIC}
behave the same on static terms.
∎

Elaboration Graduality

\mpr@inferrule\square{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}x\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}x}\mpr@inferrule\square{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\square_{i}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\square_{i}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}A\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}A^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}B\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}B^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A^{\prime}.B^{\prime}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}A\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}A^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}u\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}u^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t~u\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t^{\prime}~u^{\prime}}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{a}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}I(\mathbf{a})\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}I(\mathbf{a^{\prime}})}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{a}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{a^{\prime}}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}b}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{b^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}c(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}c(\mathbf{a^{\prime}},\mathbf{b^{\prime}})}\mpr@inferrule\square{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}a\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}a^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}P\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}P^{\prime}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{t}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\mathbf{t^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(a,z.P,\mathbf{f.y.t})\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{I}(a^{\prime},z.P^{\prime},\mathbf{f.y.t^{\prime}})}\mpr@inferrule\square{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}

Figure 11
.
Syntactic precision for
\mathsf{GCIC}

Next, we turn to elaboration graduality, the equivalent of the static gradual
guarantee (SGG) of
Siek
et al. 2015
in our setting.
We state it with respect to a notion of precision for terms in
\mathsf{GCIC}
,

syntactic precision

\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}
, defined in
Fig.

11
.
Syntactic precision is the usual and expected source-level notion of precision in gradual languages: it is generated by a single non-trivial rule
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}t\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}
, and congruence rules for all term formers.

In contrast with the simply-typed setting, the presence of multiple unknown types
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
,
one for each universe level
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}i
, requires an additional hypothesis relating elaboration
and precision.
We say that two judgments
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}
and

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
are
universe adequate
if the
universe level
j
given by the well-formedness judgment
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\operatorname{\vbox{\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright}$}}_{\square}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square_{j}
induced by correction of the elaboration satisfies
i=j
.
More generally,
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{s}
and

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
are
universe adequate
if for any
subterm
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}_{0}
of
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
inducing judgments
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}_{0}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma_{0}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}_{0}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
, those are
universe adequate.
Note that this extraneous technical assumption on universe levels is not needed if we use typical ambiguity (
Footnote

14
),
since universe levels are not given explicitly.

Theorem 22 (Elaboration Graduality / Static Gradual Guarantee).

In
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{GCIC}^{\uparrow}
, if
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{s}
and

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T
are universe adequate, then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{s}\operatorname{\rightsquigarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S
for some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S
such that
\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s
and
\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}T\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}S
.

The proof is by induction on the elaboration derivation for
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{t}
. All cases for inference consist in a straightforward combination of the hypotheses, with the universe adequacy hypothesis used in the case where
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tilde{s}
is
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}\scalebox{0.7}{@\lx@text@lbrace i\lx@text@rbrace}
.
Here again the technical difficulties arise in the rules involving reduction. This is where
Corollary

17
is useful, proving that the less structurally precise term obtained by induction in a constrained inference reduces to a less precise type. Thus either the same rule can still be used, or one has to trade a
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

,
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

or
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

rule respectively for a
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

,
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

or
9Type-directed elaboration from

GCIC

to

CastCIC

rule in case the less precise type is some
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}
and the more precise type is not. Similarly,
Corollary

18
proves that in the checking rule the less precise types are still consistent.
Note that again, because
Corollary

17
holds when restricted to weak-head reduction, elaboration graduality also holds when fixing a weak-head strategy in
Fig.

9
.
∎

Dynamic Gradual Guarantee

Following
Siek
et al. 2015
, using the fact that structural precision is
a simulation (
Theorem

16
), we can prove the DGG for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
(stated using the notion of observational refinement

\sqsubseteq^{obs}
from
Definition

4
).

Theorem 23 (Dynamic Gradual Guarantee for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
).

Suppose that
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\operatorname{\triangleright}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A
.
If moreover
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\mid\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u
then
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\sqsubseteq^{obs}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}:(\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A)\Rightarrow(\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{B})

closing over all free variables.
By the diagonal rules of structural precision, we have

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\mid\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}[t]\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}[u]
.
By progress (
Theorem

2
),
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}[t]
either reduces
to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathtt{true}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathtt{false}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\mathbb{B}}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\mathbb{B}}
or diverges, and similarly for
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}[u]
.
If
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}[t]
diverges or reduces to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\mathbb{B}}
, we are done.
If it reduces to either
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathtt{true}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathtt{false}
or
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\mathbb{B}}
, then by the catch-up
Lemma

14
,

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}[u]
either reduces to the same value, or
to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\mathbb{B}}
. In particular, it cannot diverge or reduce to
an error.
∎

Note that
Example

15
provides a counter-example to this theorem for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
, by choosing the context
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{ind}}_{\mathbb{N}}(\bullet~0,z.\mathbb{B},f.\mathtt{true},f.n.\mathtt{true})
, because in that context the function
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathbb{N}.\texttt{suc}(x)
reduces to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathtt{true}
while the less precise casted function reduces to
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\mathbb{B}}
.

As observed in
Section

2.4
,
graduality—and in particular the fact that precision induces ep-pairs—is inherently semantic, and thus cannot rely on the syntactic precision
\sqsubseteq^{\mathrm{G}}_{\alpha}
introduced in this section.
Therefore, we defer the proof of
\mathcal{G}
for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
to the next
section, where the semantic notion of
propositional precision
is introduced.

6.
Realizing
\mathsf{CastCIC}
and Graduality

To prove normalization of
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
, we now build a model of both
theories with a simple implementation of casts using case-analysis on types as well
as exceptions, yielding the
discrete model
, allowing us to reduce the
normalization of both theories to the normalization of the target theory
(
Section

6.1
).

Then, to prove graduality of
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
,
we build a more elaborate
monotone model
inducing a
precision relation well-behaved with respect to conversion.
Following generalities about the interpretation of
\mathsf{CIC}
’s types as posets
in
Section

6.2
, we describe the construction of a monotone
unknown type
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}
in
Section

6.3
and a hierarchy of
universes in
Section

6.4
and put these pieces together
in
Section

6.5
, culminating in a proof of graduality
for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
(
Section

6.6
).
In both the discrete and monotone case, the parameters
c_{\Pi}(-)
and

s_{\Pi}(-,-)

appear when building the hierarchy of universes and tying the knot with the
unknown type.

Finally, to deduce graduality for the non-terminating variant,
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
,
we describe at the end of this section a model based on

\omega
-complete partial orders, extending the seminal
model of
Scott 1976
for
\lambda
-calulus to
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
(
Section

6.7
).

The discrete model embeds into a variant of
\mathsf{CIC}
extended with
induction-recursion
(
Dybjer and Setzer 2003
)
, noted
\mathsf{CIC^{IR}}
, and the monotone model into a variant that additionally features quotients (and hence also function extensionality
(
Shulman 2011
)
), noted
\mathsf{CIC^{IR}_{QIT}}
.

Formalization in Agda

We use Agda
(
Norell 2009
)
as a practical tool to typecheck
the components of the models and assume that Agda satisfies
standard metatheoretical properties, namely subject reduction and
strong normalization.

The correspondence between the notions developed in the following
sections and the formal development in Agda
(
Lennon-Bertrand et al. 2020
)
is as follows.
The formalization covers most component of the discrete
(
DiscreteModelPartial.agda
) and monotone model
(
UnivPartial.agda
) in a partial (non-normalizing) setting and only
the discrete model is proved to be normalizing assuming normalization of the
type theory implemented by Agda (no escape hatch to termination checking is
used in
DiscreteModelTotal
).
The main definitions surrounding posets can be found in
Poset.agda
:
top and bottom elements (called
Initial
and
Final
in the
formalization), embedding-projection pairs (called
Distr
) as well as
the notions corresponding to indexed families of posets
(
IndexedPoset
, together with
IndexedDistr
). It is then
proved that we endow can the translation of each type
formers from
\mathsf{CastCIC}
with a poset structure: natural numbers in
nat.agda
, booleans in

bool.agda
, dependent product in
pi.agda
. The definition of
the monotone unknown type
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}
is defined in the subdirectory

Unknown/
. It is more involved since we need to use a
quotient (that we axiomatize together with a rewriting rule in

Unknown/Quotient.agda
).
Finally, all these building blocks are put together when assembling the
inductive-recursive hierarchies of universes (
UnivPartial.agda
,

DiscreteModelPartial.agda
and
DiscreteModelTotal.agda
).

6.1.
Discrete Model of
\mathsf{CastCIC}

The discrete model explains away the new term formers of

\mathsf{CastCIC}
(
Syntax of

CastCIC

) by a translation into
\mathsf{CIC}
using two important ingredients from the literature:

•

Exceptions, following the approach of
\mathsf{ExTT}

(
Pédrot and
Tabareau 2018
)
: each inductive type is extended with two new constructors, one for
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
and one for
\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}
.
As alluded to early on (
Section

2.5
), both
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}
are exceptional terms in their propagation semantics, and only differ in their static interpretation:
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}
A
is consistent with any other term of type
A
, while
err_A
is not consistent with any such term.

•

Case analysis on types
(
Boulier
et al. 2017
)
to define the cast operator. The
essence of the translation is to interpret types as
codes
when they are
seen as terms, and as
the semantics of those codes
when they are seen as
types.
This allows us to get the standard interpretation for a term inhabiting
a type, but at the same time, it allows functions taking terms in the universe

\square\!\!\!\!\square{}_{i}
to perform a case analysis on the code of the type, because this
time, the type is seen as a term in
\square\!\!\!\!\square{}_{i}
.

The latter ingredient for intensional type
analysis requires the target theory of the translation to be an extension of

\mathsf{CIC}
with induction-recursion
(
Dybjer and Setzer 2003
)
, noted
\mathsf{CIC^{IR}}
. We write
\leadsto_{\mathrm{IR}}

and
\vdash_{\mathrm{IR}}
to denote the reduction and typing judgments of
\mathsf{CIC^{IR}}
,
respectively.

Inductive types

Following the general pattern of
\mathsf{ExTT}
, we interpret each inductive type
I
by
an inductive type
\ddot{I}
featuring all constructors of
I

and extended with two new constructors

\top_{\ddot{I}}
and
\bot_{\ddot{I}}
, corresponding respectively to

{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{I}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{I}
of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
.
The constructors
\top_{\ddot{I}}
and
\bot_{\ddot{I}}
of

\ddot{I}
are called
exceptional
by opposition to the other
constructors that we call
non-exceptional
.
For instance, the inductive type used to interpret natural numbers,

\ddot{\mathbb{N}}{}
, thus has 4 constructors: the non-exceptional constructors
0

and
suc
, and the exceptional
constructors
\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\bot_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}
.
In the rest of this section, we only illustrate inductive types on natural numbers.

Universe and type-case

Case analysis on types is obtained through an explicit inductive-recursive description of
the universes
(
Martin-Löf 1984
;
McBride 2010
)
to build a type of codes
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}

described in
Fig.

12
.
Codes are noted with
\widehat{\cdot}
and the universe type contains codes for
dependent product (
\widehat{\Pi}
), universes (
\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{j}
), inductive types (
e.g.,

\widehat{\mathbb{N}}
) as well as
\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}
for the unknown type and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}}
for the error type. The main subtlety here is that the code
\widehat{\Pi}\,A\,B
is at level
s_{\Pi}(i,j)

when
A
is at
i
and
B
is a family at
j
, emulating the rule of

Fig.

3
.
Accompanying the inductive definition of
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
, the recursively defined
decoding function
\mathrm{El}
provides a semantics for these codes.
The semantics of
\widehat{\Pi}
is given by the dependent product in the target theory,
applying
\mathrm{El}
on the domain and the codomain of the code.
The semantics of
\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{j}
is precisely the type of codes
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{j}
.
The semantics of
\widehat{\mathbb{N}}
is given by the extended natural numbers
\ddot{\mathbb{N}}
, explained above.

Intuitively, the semantics of
\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
is that an inhabitant of
the unknown type corresponds to a pair of a type and an inhabitant of
that type. More precisely, we first define a notion of germ for codes where we stratify the head constructors
\operatorname{Head}
(see
Fig.

4
) according to the
universe level
i
, e.g.

\widehat{\operatorname{germ}}_{i}\,\Pi:=\widehat{\Pi}\,\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{c_{\Pi}(i)}\,(\lambda(x:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{c_{\Pi}(i)}).\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{c_{\Pi}(i)})
when
c_{\Pi}(i)\geq 0
,
and its decoding to types

\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h:=\mathrm{El}\,(\widehat{\operatorname{germ}}_{i}\,h)
.
The unknown type
\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
is then decoded to the extended dependent sum

{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,\operatorname{Head}_{i}\,\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,
whose elements are
either:

•

one of the two freely added constructors
\top_{{\ddot{\Sigma}}},\bot_{{\ddot{\Sigma}}}
following the interpretation scheme of inductive types;

•

or a dependent pair
({h};{t})
of a head constructor
h\in\operatorname{Head}_{i}

together with an element
t\in\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h
.

Finally, the error type
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{i}}
is decoded to the unit type
\mathtt{unit}
containing a unique element

\mathtt{()}
.

\mpr@inferrule\square{A\in\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}\\
B\in\mathrm{El}\,A\to\square\!\!\!\!\square_{j}}{\widehat{\Pi}\,A\,B\in\square\!\!\!\!\square_{s_{\Pi}(i,j)}}\mpr@inferrule\square{j<i}{\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{j}\in\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}\in\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}\in\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{i}}\in\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}

\displaystyle=\Pi(a:\mathrm{El}\,A)~\mathrm{El}(B\,a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{j}

\displaystyle=\square\!\!\!\!\square_{j}

\displaystyle\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle=\ddot{\mathbb{N}}{}

\displaystyle\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}

\displaystyle={\ddot{\Sigma}}\,\operatorname{Head}_{i}\,\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{i}}

Variants of
\mathsf{CastCIC}

Crucially, the code for
\Pi
-types (
Fig.

12
) depends on the choice made for
s_{\Pi}(i,j)
.
Observe that for the choice of parameters corresponding to
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
, the
inductive-recursive definition of
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
is ill-founded since

c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,i))=s_{\Pi}(i,i)
. We can thus inject

\operatorname{germ}{}_{s_{\Pi}(i,i)}\Pi=\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}{s_{\Pi}(i,i)}\to\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}{s_{\Pi}(i,i)}
into
\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}{s_{\Pi}(i,i)}
and project
back in the other direction, exhibiting an embedding-retraction
suitable to interpret the untyped
\lambda
-calculus and hence
\Omega
.
17
17

17

In the Agda implementation, we deactivate the termination checker
on the definition of the universe for the model interpreting
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
,
thus effectively working in a partial, inconsistent type theory.

In order to maintain normalization, the construction of the unknown type and the
universe therefore needs to be stratified, which is possible when

c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,i))<s_{\Pi}(i,i)
.
This strict inequality occurs for both
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
.
We then proceed by strong induction on the universe level, and note that thanks to
the level gap, the decoding
\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
of the unknown type at a level
i

can be defined solely from the data of smaller universes available by inductive
hypothesis, without any reference to
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
.
We can then define the rest of the universe
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
and the decoding function

\mathrm{El}
at level
i
in a well-founded manner, validating the strict positivity
criterion of Agda’s termination checker.

\displaystyle{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\widehat{\Pi}{}\,A\,B}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\lambda}x:\mathrm{El}\,A.{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\mathrm{El}\,(B\,x)}

\displaystyle{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{j}}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{j}

\displaystyle{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}

\displaystyle:=\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}

\displaystyle{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{j}}

\displaystyle:=\top_{\mathrm{El}\,\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{j}}

\displaystyle{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{j}}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{j}}}

\displaystyle:=\mathtt{()}

Exceptions

The definition of exceptions
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{A},\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{A}:\mathrm{El}~A
at an arbitrary code
A
then
follows by case analysis on the code, as shown in
Fig.

13
.
On the code for the universe,
\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{j}
, we directly use the code for the unknown and the error
types respectively.
On codes that have an inductive interpretation—
\widehat{\mathbb{N}}
,

\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
—we use the two added constructors.
On the code for dependent functions, exceptions are defined by re-raising the exception at the
codomain in a pointwise fashion.
Finally, on the error type
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}}
, exceptions are degenerated and
forced to take the only value
\mathtt{()}:\mathtt{unit}
18
18

18

This definition is indeed uniform if

\mathtt{unit}
is seen as the record type with no projection.
of its interpretation as a
type.

Definition of

Casts

Equipped with exceptions and type analysis, we define

\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}:\operatorname{\Pi}(A:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i})(B:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{j}).A\to B
by
induction on the universe levels and case analysis on the codes of the types

A
and
B
(
Fig.

14
).
In the total setting (when
c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,i))<s_{\Pi}(i,i)
), the definition of
\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}
is well-founded: each recursive
call happens either at a strictly smaller universe (the two cases for

\widehat{\Pi}
) or on a strict subterm of the term being cast (case of
inductives,
i.e.,

\widehat{\mathbb{N}}
and
\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}
).
Note that each of the defining equations of
\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}
corresponds straightforwardly to a
reduction rule of
Fig.

5
.

Discrete translation

We can finally define the discrete syntactic model of
\mathsf{CastCIC}
in
\mathsf{CIC^{IR}}
(
Fig.

15
).
The translations
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}-}]
and
\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}-}\rrbracket{}
are defined by induction on the syntax of
terms and types.
A type
A
is translated to its corresponding code
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}]

in
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
when seen as a term, and is translated to the interpretation
of this code
\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}\rrbracket{}:=\mathrm{El}\,[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}]
when seen as a type.

{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{A}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{A}
are directly translated using the exceptions
defined in
Fig.

13
.
The following theorem shows that the translation is a syntactic model in the sense
of
Boulier
et al. 2017
.

\begin{array}[t]{lcl}\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\cdot}\rrbracket&:=&\cdot\\[6.00006pt]
\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}\rrbracket&:=&\mathrm{El}\,[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}]\\
\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}x}]}&:=&x\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\square{}_{i}}]}&:=&\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}_{i}\\[6.00006pt]
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\Pi}x:A.B}]}&:=&\widehat{\Pi}~[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}]~(\operatorname{\lambda}x:\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}\rrbracket{}.[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}B}])\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t~u}]}&:=&[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}]~[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u}]\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\lambda}x:A.t}]}&:=&\operatorname{\lambda}x:\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}\rrbracket{}.[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}]\end{array}\begin{array}[t]{lcl}\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma,x:A}\rrbracket&:=&\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma}\rrbracket{},x:\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}\rrbracket\\[6.00006pt]
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathbb{N}}]}&:=&\widehat{\mathbb{N}}\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}0}]}&:=&0\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\texttt{suc}}]}&:=&\texttt{suc}\\
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathtt{ind}_{\mathbb{N}}}]~P~h_{0}\,h_{\texttt{suc}}&:=&\mathtt{ind}_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}{}}~P~h_{0}\,h_{\texttt{suc}}\,{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{P\,{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}{}}}\,\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{(P\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}{}})}\\[6.00006pt]
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{A}}]}&:=&{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}]}\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{A}}]}&:=&\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}]}\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{B}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{A}\rangle\,{t}}]}&:=&\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}\,[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}]\,[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}B}]\,[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}]\end{array}

Figure 15
.
Discrete translation from
\mathsf{CastCIC}
to
\mathsf{CIC^{IR}}

Theorem 1 (Discrete syntactic model).

The translation defined in
Fig.

15

preserves conversion and typing derivations:

(1)

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t\leadsto{}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u
then
\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma}\rrbracket{}\vdash_{\mathrm{IR}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}]\leadsto^{+}_{\mathrm{IR}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u}]
, in particular
\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma}\rrbracket{}\vdash_{\mathrm{IR}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}]\equiv[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u}]
,

(2)

if
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t:\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A
then
\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma}\rrbracket{}\vdash_{\mathrm{IR}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}]:\llbracket{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A}\rrbracket{}
.

(1) All reduction rules from
\mathsf{CIC}
are preserved without a
change so that we only need to be concerned with the reduction rules involving
exceptions or casts.
A careful inspection shows that these reductions are preserved too once we
observe that the terms of the shape

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\langle{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\square_{i}}}{\ \Leftarrow\ }{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}\rangle\,{t}
that are stuck in
\mathsf{CastCIC}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the one-step reduced form of its
translation
({h};{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t}]}):{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,\operatorname{Head}_{i}\,\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,
.
(2) Proved by a direct induction on the typing derivation of

\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Gamma\vdash_{\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}t:\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A
, using the fact that exceptions and casts are well-typed—that is
\vdash_{\mathrm{IR}}{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}:\operatorname{\Pi}(A:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i})\,\mathrm{El}\,A
,
\vdash_{\mathrm{IR}}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}:\operatorname{\Pi}(A:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i})\,\mathrm{El}\,A
, and
\vdash_{\mathrm{IR}}\operatorname{\mathtt{cast}}:\operatorname{\Pi}(A:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i})(B:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i})\mathrm{El}\,A{\to}\mathrm{El}\,B
—and relying on assertion
(1)
to handle the
conversion rule.
∎

As explained in
Theorem

3
,
Theorem

1

implies in particular that
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
are strongly
normalizing.

6.2.
Poset-Based Models of Dependent Type Theory

The simplicity of the discrete model comes at the price of an inherent inability to
characterize which casts are guaranteed to succeed,
i.e.,
a graduality theorem.
To overcome this limitation, we develop a monotone model on top of the discrete
model where, by construction, each type
A
comes equipped with an order
structure
\sqsubseteq^{A}
—a reflexive, transitive, antisymmetric and
proof-irrelevant relation—modelling precision between terms.
In particular, the exceptions
\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{A}
and
{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{A}
correspond respectively to the smallest
and greatest element of
A
for this order.
We note
\square^{\leq}
for a universe of types equipped with the structure of a poset
together with smallest and greatest elements.
Each term and type constructor is enforced to be monotone with respect to these orders,
providing a strong form of graduality.
This implies in particular that such a model cannot be defined for
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
because this type theory lacks graduality, as shown by
Example

15
.

As an illustration, the order on extended natural numbers (
Fig.

16
) makes

\bot_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}
the smallest element and
\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}
the biggest element.
19
19

19

We abusively note
\ddot{\mathbb{N}}
for both
the poset and its carrier to avoid introducing too many notations.

The standard natural numbers –
0
or
\texttt{suc}~n
for a standard natural
number
n
– then stand between failure and indeterminacy,
but are never related to each other by precision. Indeed, in order to ensure conservativity with respect to
\mathsf{CIC}
,
\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}}
must coincide with
\mathsf{CIC}
’s conversion on static closed natural numbers.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}0

\displaystyle\bot_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}n

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq^{{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}}\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}

\displaystyle\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq^{{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}}\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}

\mpr@inferrule\square{n\sqsubseteq^{{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}}m}{\texttt{suc}\,n\sqsubseteq^{{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}}\texttt{suc}\,m}\mpr@inferrule\square{n\sqsubseteq^{{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}}\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}}{\texttt{suc}\,n\sqsubseteq^{{\widehat{\mathbb{N}}}}\top_{\ddot{\mathbb{N}}}}

Figure 16
.
Order structure on extended natural numbers

Beyond the precision order on types, the nature of dependency forces us to spell
out what the precision between types entails.
Following the analysis of
New and Ahmed 2018
,
a relation
A\sqsubseteq B
between types should
induce an embedding-projection pair (ep-pair): a pair of an
upcast

{\uparrow{}:A{\to}B}
and a
downcast

{\downarrow{}:B{\to}A}

satisfying a handful of properties with gradual guarantees as a
corollary.

Definition 0 (Embedding-projection pairs).

An ep-pair
d:A\vartriangleleft{}B
between posets
A,B:\square^{\leq}
consists of

•

an underlying relation
d\subseteq A\times B
such that

a^{\prime}\sqsubseteq^{A}a\>\wedge\>d(a,b)\>\wedge\>b\sqsubseteq^{B}b^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad d(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})

•

that is bi-represented by
\uparrow_{d}~:A\to B
,
\downarrow{}_{d}:B\to A
,
i.e.,

\uparrow_{d}a\sqsubseteq^{B}b\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad d(a,b)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad a\sqsubseteq^{A}\;\downarrow_{d}b,

•

such that the equality
\downarrow_{d}\circ\uparrow_{d}~=\mathrm{id}_{A}
holds.

Note that here equiprecision of the retraction becomes an equality
because of antisymmetry.
Under these conditions,
\uparrow_{d}:A\hookrightarrow B
is injective,

\downarrow_{d}:B\twoheadrightarrow A
is surjective and both preserve bottom
elements, explaining that we call
d:A\vartriangleleft{}B
an embedding-projection
pair.
The definition of ep-pairs is based on a relation rather than just its
pair of representing functions to highlight the connection between ep-pairs and
parametricity
(
New
et al. 2020
)
.
Assuming function extensionality, being an ep-pair is a
property of the underlying relation: there is at most one pair
(\uparrow{}_{d},\downarrow_{d})
representing the underlying relation of
d
.
An ep-pair straightforwardly induces the following
relations that will be used in later proofs.

Lemma 3 (Properties of ep-pairs).

Let
d:A\vartriangleleft{}B
be an ep-pair between posets.

(1)

If
a:A
then
d\,(a,\uparrow_{d}a)
and
a\sqsubseteq^{A}\downarrow_{d}\uparrow_{d}a
.

(2)

If
b:B
then
d\,(\downarrow_{d}b,b)
and
\uparrow_{d}\downarrow_{d}b\sqsubseteq^{B}b
.

Posetal families

By monotonicity, a family
B:A\to\square^{\leq}
over a poset
A
gives rise not
only to a poset
B\,a
for each
a\in A
, but also to ep-pairs
B_{a,a^{\prime}}:B\,a\vartriangleleft{}B\,a^{\prime}
for each
a\sqsubseteq^{A}a^{\prime}
.
These ep-pairs need to satisfy functoriality conditions:

B_{a,a}={\sqsubseteq^{B\,a}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{a,a^{\prime\prime}}=B_{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}}\circ B_{a,a^{\prime}}\quad\text{whenever}\quad a\sqsubseteq^{A}a^{\prime}\sqsubseteq^{A}a^{\prime\prime}.

In particular, this ensures that heterogeneous transitivity is well
defined:

B_{a,a^{\prime}}(b,b^{\prime})\wedge B_{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\Rightarrow B_{a,a^{\prime\prime}}(b,b^{\prime\prime}).

Dependent products

Given a poset
A
and a posetal family
B
over
A
, we can form the poset

\operatorname{\Pi}^{\text{mon}}{}\,A\,B
of
monotone
dependent functions from
A
to
B
,
equipped with the pointwise order.
Its inhabitants are dependent functions
f:\operatorname{\Pi}(a:A).B\,a
such that
a\sqsubseteq^{A}a^{\prime}{\Rightarrow}B_{a,a^{\prime}}\,(f\,a,f\,a^{\prime})
.
Moreover, given ep-pairs
d_{A}:A\vartriangleleft{}A^{\prime}
and
d_{B}:B\vartriangleleft{}B^{\prime}
, we
can build an induced ep-pair
d_{\Pi}:\operatorname{\Pi}^{\text{mon}}{}\,A\,B\vartriangleleft{}\operatorname{\Pi}^{\text{mon}}{}\,A^{\prime}\,B^{\prime}

with underlying relation

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}(f,f^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=d_{A}(a,a^{\prime})\Rightarrow d_{B}(f\,a,f^{\prime}\,a^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\uparrow{}_{d_{\Pi}}f:={\uparrow_{d_{B}}}\circ f\circ\downarrow{}_{d_{A}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\downarrow{}_{d_{\Pi}}f:={\downarrow_{d_{B}}}\circ f\circ\uparrow{}_{d_{A}}.

The general case where
B
and
B^{\prime}
actually depend on
A,A^{\prime}
is obtained with
similar formulas, but a larger amount of data is required to handle
the dependency: we refer to the accompanying Agda development for details.

Inductive types

Generalizing the case of natural numbers, the order on an arbitrary extended
inductive type
\ddot{I}
uses the following scheme:

(1)

\bot_{\ddot{I}}
is the least element

(2)

\top_{\ddot{I}}\sqsubseteq^{\ddot{I}}\top_{\ddot{I}}

(3)

c~\mathbf{t}\sqsubseteq^{\ddot{I}}\top_{\ddot{I}}
whenever
t_{i}\sqsubseteq^{X_{i}}\top_{X_{i}}
for
all
i

(4)

each constructor
c
is monotone with respect to the order on its arguments

The precondition on subterms in the third case is unnecessary in simple cases
and is kept to be uniform with definition of order on the monotone unknown type
in the following section.

Similarly to dependent product, an ep-pair
\mathbf{X}\vartriangleleft{}\mathbf{X^{\prime}}

between the parameters of an extended inductive type
\ddot{I}
induces an ep-pair

\ddot{I}\,\mathbf{X}\vartriangleleft{}\ddot{I}\,\mathbf{X^{\prime}}
.
For instance, ep-pairs
d_{A}:A\vartriangleleft{}A^{\prime}
and
d_{B}:B\vartriangleleft{}B^{\prime}
induce
an ep-pair
d_{{\ddot{\Sigma}}}:{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,A\,B\vartriangleleft{}{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,A^{\prime}\,B^{\prime}

defined by
d_{{\ddot{\Sigma}}}((a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})):=d_{A}(a,a^{\prime})\wedge d_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
.

6.3.
Microcosm: the Monotone Unknown Type
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}

The interpretation
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
of the unknown type in the monotone model should
morally group together approximations of every type at the same universe
level.
Working in (bi)pointed orders,
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
can be realized as a coalesced sum

(
Abramsky and Jung 1995
, section 3.2.3)
of
the family
\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h
indexed by head constructors
h\in\operatorname{Head}_{i}
.
A concrete presentation of
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
is obtained as
the quotient of
{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,\operatorname{Head}_{i}\,\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,

identifying
\bot_{{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,\operatorname{Head}_{i}\,\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,}
with any pair

({h};{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\widehat{\operatorname{germ}}_{i}\,h}})
.
The equivalence classes of
({h};{x})
is noted as
[{h};{x}]
,

\bot_{{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,\operatorname{Head}_{i}\,\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,}
as
\bot_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
and

\top_{{\ddot{\Sigma}}\,\operatorname{Head}_{i}\,\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,}
as
\top_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
.
The obtained type
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
is then equipped
with a precision relation defined by the rules:

(1)

\displaystyle\bot_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}z

\displaystyle\top_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}\top_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}

\displaystyle\mpr@inferrule\square{x\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{\operatorname{germ}}_{i}\,h}x^{\prime}}{[{h};{x}]\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}[{h};{x^{\prime}}]}

\displaystyle\mpr@inferrule\square{}{[{h};{x}]\sqsubseteq^{\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}\top_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}}

These rules ensure that the exceptions

\bot_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
and
\top_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
are
respectively the smallest and biggest elements of
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
.
Non-exceptional elements
are comparable only if they have the same head constructor
h
and if so are
compared according to the interpretation of that head constructor as an ordered
type
\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h
.
Because of the quotient, it is not immediate that this presentation of

\sqsubseteq^{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
is independent of the choice of representatives in
equivalence classes and that it forms a proof-irrelevant relation.
In the formal development, we define the relation by quotient-induction on each argument, thus verifying that it respects the quotient, and also show that
it is irrelevant. This relies crucially on equality being decidable
on head constructors when comparing
[{h};{x}]
and
[{h^{\prime}};{x^{\prime}}]
.

In order to globally satisfy
\mathcal{G}
,
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
should admit an ep-pair
{d_{h}:\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h\vartriangleleft{}\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
whenever we have a head constructor
h\in\operatorname{Head}_{i}
such that
\widehat{\operatorname{germ}}_{i}\,h\sqsubseteq\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
(we return to that
point in the next section
Section

6.4
).
Embedding an element
x\in\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h
by
\uparrow_{d_{h}}x=[{h};{x}]

and projecting out of
\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h
by the following equations form a reasonable
candidate:

\displaystyle\downarrow_{d_{h}}[{h^{\prime}};{x}]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x&if $h=h^{\prime}$\\
\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}&otherwise\end{cases}

\displaystyle\downarrow_{d_{h}}\top_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}

\displaystyle={\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}_{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}

\displaystyle\downarrow_{d_{h}}\bot_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}

Note that we rely again on
\operatorname{Head}
having decidable equality to compute the

\downarrow{}_{d_{h}}
.
Moreover
\uparrow_{d_{h}}\dashv\downarrow_{d_{h}}
should be adjoints; in particular,
the following precision relation needs to hold:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}\sqsubseteq^{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}{\downarrow{}_{d_{h}}\bot_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}}\quad\iff\quad[{h};{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}}]={\uparrow_{d_{h}}\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}}\sqsubseteq^{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}\bot_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}

Since
\sqsubseteq^{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
should be antisymmetric, this is possible only
if
[{h};{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\operatorname{germ}_{i}\,h}}]
and
\bot_{\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
are
identified in
\ddot{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
, explaining why we have to quotient in the first place.

6.4.
Realization of the Monotone Universe Hierarchy

\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}

and decoding function

\mathrm{El}\,:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}\to\square^{\leq}

(cases distinct from

)

.
Monotone universe of codes and precision

Following the discrete model, the monotone universe hierarchy is
also implemented through an inductive-recursive datatype of codes
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}

together with a decoding function
\mathrm{El}:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}\to\square
, both presented in

Fig.

17
.
The precision relation
{\sqsubseteq}:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}\to\square\!\!\!\!\square_{j}\to\square

presented below is an
order (
Theorem

4
) on this universe hierarchy.
The “diagonal” inference rules, providing evidence for relating type
constructors from
\mathsf{CIC}
, coincide with those of binary
parametricity
(
Bernardy
et al. 2012
)
.
Outside the diagonal,
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}}
is placed at the bottom.
More interestingly, the derivation of a precision proof
A\sqsubseteq\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}
provides a unique decomposition of
A
through iterated germs
directed by the relevant head constructors.
For instance, in the gradual systems
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\uparrow}
where the equation
c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,j))=\max(i,j)
holds for any universe levels
i,j
, the derivation of
(\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}{\to}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}){\to}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}\sqsubseteq\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}
canonically decomposes as:

(\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}{\to}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}){\to}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}\quad\sqsubseteq\quad(\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}{\to}\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}){\to}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}\quad\sqsubseteq\quad\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}{\to}\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}\quad\sqsubseteq\quad\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}

This unique decomposition is at the heart of the reduction of the cast
operator given in
Fig.

5
, and it can be described informally
as taking the path of maximal length between two related types.
20
20

20

This
decomposition is already present in
(
New and Ahmed 2018
)
and
to be contrasted with the AGT approach
(
Garcia
et al. 2016
)
, which tends
to pair a value with the most precise witness of its type,
i.e.,
canonical path of minimal length.

Such a derivation of precision
A\sqsubseteq B
gives rise through decoding to
ep-pairs
\mathrm{El}{}_{\varepsilon}\,(A{\sqsubseteq}B):\mathrm{El}\,A\vartriangleleft{}\mathrm{El}\,B
, with underlying relation
noted
{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}\sqsubseteq^{{\kern-20.39735pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern .
This decoding function

is described on generators of

\mathrm{El}{}_{\varepsilon}(\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}}\text{-}{\sqsubseteq})
states that the unique value
\mathtt{()}

is smaller than any other value.
The diagonal cases

and
\mathrm{El}{}_{\varepsilon}(\widehat{\square\!\!\!\!\square}\text{-}\sqsubseteq)
reuse
the order specified on the carrier.
The ep-pair
\mathrm{El}{}_{\varepsilon}(\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}\text{-}{\sqsubseteq})

\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}}
and

and greater than any other value, and that the comparison between two injected
terms with same head is induced by their second component.

.

It is interesting to observe what happens in
\mathsf{CastCIC}^{\mathcal{N}}
,
where

c_{\Pi}(s_{\Pi}(i,j))\neq\max(i,j)
, for instance on the previous example:

\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}{\to}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}\quad\not\sqsubseteq\quad\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}}=\widehat{\operatorname{germ}}_{0}\,\Pi\quad\sqsubseteq\quad\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}

So
\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}{\to}\widehat{\mathbb{N}}{}
is not lower than
\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}
in that setting.

One crucial point of the monotone model is the mutual definition of codes
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}

together with the precision relation, particularly salient on codes for

\Pi
-types: in
\widehat{\Pi}\,A\,B
,
B:\mathrm{El}\,A\to\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
is a monotone function with
respect to the order on
\mathrm{El}\,A
and the precision on
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
.
This intertwining happens because the order is required to be reflexive, a fact
observed previously by
Atkey
et al. 2014
in the similar setting of reflexive
graphs.
Indeed, a dependent function
f:\Pi(a:\mathrm{El}\,A).\,\mathrm{El}\,(B\,a)
is related to
itself
f\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.2858pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.2858pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[15.52641pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.34425pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{\Pi}{}\,A\,B}}}}\sqsubseteq^{{\kern-61.83292pt\mathchoice{\makebox[20.2858pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.2858pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[15.52641pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.34425pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.52625pt\mathchoice{\makebox[20.2858pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.2858pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[15.52641pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.34425pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-24.16377pt{\widehat{\Pi}{}\,A\,B}\kern 4.99846pt{\widehat{\Pi}{}\,A\,B}}}}f
if and only if
f
is

monotone
.

Theorem 4 (Properties of the universe hierarchy).

(1)

\sqsubseteq
is reflexive, transitive, antisymmetric and irrelevant so
that
(\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i},\sqsubseteq)
is a poset.

(2)

\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
has a bottom element
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathtt{err}}_{i}}
and a top element
\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
; in
particular,
A\sqsubseteq\widehat{{\operatorname{\boldsymbol{?}}}}_{i}
for any
A:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
.

(3)

\mathrm{El}:\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}\to\square
is a family of posets over
\square\!\!\!\!\square_{i}
with underlying relation

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}\sqsubseteq^{{\kern-20.39735pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.52625pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.31236pt{A}\kern 8.52625pt{B}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}\sqsubseteq^{{\kern-20.39735pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern
